href="/wiki/Margaret_of_Wessex" class="mw-redirect" title="Margaret of Wessex">Margaret of Wessex</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>David I</b> or <b>Dauíd mac Maíl Choluim</b> (<a href="/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Scottish Gaelic language">Modern</a>: <i>Daibhidh I mac [Mhaoil] Chaluim</i>;<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1084</span> – 24 May 1153) was a 12th-century ruler who was <a href="/wiki/David,_Prince_of_the_Cumbrians" class="mw-redirect" title="David, Prince of the Cumbrians">Prince of the Cumbrians</a> from 1113 to 1124 and later <a href="/wiki/King_of_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="King of Scotland">King of Scotland</a> from 1124 to 1153. The youngest son of <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Malcolm III">Malcolm III</a> and <a href="/wiki/Margaret_of_Wessex" class="mw-redirect" title="Margaret of Wessex">Margaret of Wessex</a>, David spent most of his childhood in <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a>, but was exiled to <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a> temporarily in 1093. Perhaps after 1100, he became a dependent at the court of King <a href="/wiki/Henry_I_of_England" title="Henry I of England">Henry I</a>. There he was influenced by the Anglo-French culture of the court.
</p><p>When David's brother <a href="/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Scotland" title="Alexander I of Scotland">Alexander I</a> died in 1124, David chose, with the backing of Henry I, to take the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland" title="Kingdom of Scotland">Kingdom of Scotland</a> (<a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Alba" title="Kingdom of Alba">Alba</a>) for himself. He was forced to engage in warfare against his rival and nephew, <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A1el_Coluim_mac_Alaxandair" title="Máel Coluim mac Alaxandair">Máel Coluim mac Alaxandair</a>. Subduing the latter seems to have taken David ten years, a struggle that involved the destruction of <a href="/wiki/%C3%93engus_of_Moray" title="Óengus of Moray">Óengus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mormaer_of_Moray" class="mw-redirect" title="Mormaer of Moray">Mormaer of Moray</a>. David's victory allowed expansion of control over more distant regions theoretically part of his Kingdom. After the death of his former patron Henry I, David supported the claims of Henry's daughter and his own niece, <a href="/wiki/Empress_Matilda" title="Empress Matilda">Empress Matilda</a>, to the throne of England. In the process, he came into conflict with King <a href="/wiki/Stephen_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Stephen of England">Stephen</a> and was able to expand his power in northern England, despite his defeat at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Standard" title="Battle of the Standard">Battle of the Standard</a> in 1138. David I is a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>, with his <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> celebrated on 24 May.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The term "<a href="/wiki/Davidian_Revolution" title="Davidian Revolution">Davidian Revolution</a>" is used by many scholars to summarise the changes that took place in Scotland during his reign. These included his foundation of <a href="/wiki/Burgh" title="Burgh">burghs</a> and regional markets, implementation of the ideals of <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_Reform" title="Gregorian Reform">Gregorian Reform</a>, foundation of <a href="/wiki/Monastery" title="Monastery">monasteries</a>, Normanisation of the Scottish government, and the introduction of <a href="/wiki/Feudalism" title="Feudalism">feudalism</a> through immigrant <a href="/wiki/French_people" title="French people">French</a> and <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Norman_language" title="Anglo-Norman language">Anglo-French</a> knights.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_years">Early years</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early years">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>David was born on a date unknown in 1084 in Scotland.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He was probably the eighth son of <a href="/wiki/King_Malcolm_III" class="mw-redirect" title="King Malcolm III">King Malcolm III</a>, and certainly the sixth and youngest borne by Malcolm's second wife, <a href="/wiki/Margaret_of_Wessex" class="mw-redirect" title="Margaret of Wessex">Margaret of Wessex</a>. He was the grandson of <a href="/wiki/King_Duncan_I" class="mw-redirect" title="King Duncan I">King Duncan I</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1093, King Malcolm and David's brother Edward were killed at the <a href="/wiki/River_Aln" title="River Aln">River Aln</a> during an invasion of <a href="/wiki/Northumberland" title="Northumberland">Northumberland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> David and his two brothers <a href="/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Scotland" title="Alexander I of Scotland">Alexander</a> and <a href="/wiki/Edgar_of_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="Edgar of Scotland">Edgar</a> were probably present when their mother died shortly afterwards.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> According to later medieval tradition, the three brothers were in <a href="/wiki/Edinburgh" title="Edinburgh">Edinburgh</a> when they were besieged by their paternal uncle <a href="/wiki/Donald_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Donald III">Donald III</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> who made himself king.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> It is not certain what happened next, but an insertion in the <i><a href="/wiki/Chronicle_of_Melrose" title="Chronicle of Melrose">Chronicle of Melrose</a></i> states that Donald forced his three nephews into exile, although he was allied with another of his nephews, <a href="/wiki/Edmund_of_Scotland" title="Edmund of Scotland">Edmund</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> John of Fordun wrote, centuries later, that an escort into England was arranged for them by their maternal uncle <a href="/wiki/Edgar_%C3%86theling" title="Edgar Ætheling">Edgar Ætheling</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:William2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/William2.jpg/170px-William2.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="243" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/William2.jpg/255px-William2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/22/William2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="278" data-file-height="397" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:William2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/William_II_of_England" title="William II of England">William</a> "Rufus", the Red, King of the English, and partial instigator of the Scottish civil war, 1093–1097.</div></div></div>
<p>King <a href="/wiki/William_Rufus" class="mw-redirect" title="William Rufus">William Rufus</a> of England opposed Donald's accession to the northerly kingdom. He sent the eldest son of Malcolm, David's half-brother <a href="/wiki/Duncan_II_of_Scotland" title="Duncan II of Scotland">Duncan</a>, into Scotland with an army. Duncan was killed within the year,<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> and so in 1097 William sent Duncan's half-brother Edgar into Scotland. The latter was more successful, and was crowned by the end of 1097.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the power struggle of 1093–97, David was in England. In 1093, he may have been about nine years old.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> From 1093 until 1103 David's presence cannot be accounted for in detail, but he appears to have been in Scotland for the remainder of the 1090s. When William Rufus was killed, his brother <a href="/wiki/Henry_Beauclerc" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry Beauclerc">Henry Beauclerc</a> seized power and married David's sister, <a href="/wiki/Matilda_of_Scotland" title="Matilda of Scotland">Matilda</a>. The marriage made David the brother-in-law of the ruler of England. From that point onwards, David was probably an important figure at the English court.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Despite his Gaelic background, by the end of his stay in England, David had become a fully Normanised prince. <a href="/wiki/William_of_Malmesbury" title="William of Malmesbury">William of Malmesbury</a> wrote that it was in this period that David "rubbed off all tarnish of Scottish barbarity through being polished by intercourse and friendship with us".<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Early_rule_1113.E2.80.931124"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_rule_1113–1124">Early rule 1113–1124</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early rule 1113–1124">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/David_I_as_Prince_of_the_Cumbrians" title="David I as Prince of the Cumbrians">David I as Prince of the Cumbrians</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Prince_of_the_Cumbrians">Prince of the Cumbrians</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Prince of the Cumbrians">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:DavidianCumbria-en.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/DavidianCumbria-en.svg/220px-DavidianCumbria-en.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="190" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/DavidianCumbria-en.svg/330px-DavidianCumbria-en.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/DavidianCumbria-en.svg/440px-DavidianCumbria-en.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="539" data-file-height="465" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:DavidianCumbria-en.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Map of David's principality of "the Cumbrians"<sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This image needs references to reliable sources (December 2022)">image reference needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></div></div></div>
<p>David's brother King Edgar had visited William Rufus in May 1099 and bequeathed to David extensive territory to the south of the <a href="/wiki/River_Forth" title="River Forth">river Forth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> On 8 January 1107, Edgar died. His younger brother Alexander took the throne. It has been assumed that David took control of his <a href="/wiki/Inheritance" title="Inheritance">inheritance</a> – the southern lands bequeathed by Edgar – soon after the latter's death.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> However, it cannot be shown that he possessed his inheritance until his foundation of <a href="/wiki/Selkirk_Abbey" class="mw-redirect" title="Selkirk Abbey">Selkirk Abbey</a> late in 1113.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Richard_Oram" title="Richard Oram">Richard Oram</a>, it was only in 1113, when Henry returned to England from Normandy, that David was at last in a position to claim his inheritance in southern Scotland.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>King Henry's backing seems to have been enough to force King Alexander to recognise his younger brother's claims. This probably occurred without bloodshed, but through threat of force nonetheless.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> David's aggression seems to have inspired resentment amongst some native Scots. A <a href="/wiki/Middle_Gaelic" class="mw-redirect" title="Middle Gaelic">Middle Gaelic</a> quatrain from this period complains that:
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1123665981">.mw-parser-output .verse_translation .translated{padding-left:2em}@media only screen and (max-width:43.75em){.mw-parser-output .verse_translation.wrap_when_small td{display:block;padding-left:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .verse_translation.wrap_when_small .translated{padding-left:0.5em}}</style>
</p>
<table role="presentation" class="verse_translation wrap_when_small" style="margin-left:1em !important;">

<tbody><tr style="vertical-align: top;">
<td><div style="font-style:italic;text-align:left" lang="mga" class="poem">
<p>Olc a ndearna mac Mael Colaim,<br />
ar cosaid re hAlaxandir,<br />
do-ní le gach mac rígh romhaind,<br />
foghail ar faras Albain.
</p>
</div>
</td>
<td class="translated"><div class="poem">
<p>It's bad what Máel Coluim's son has done;,<br />
dividing us from Alexander;<br />
he causes, like each king's son before;<br />
the plunder of stable Alba.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
</div>
</td></tr>
<tr style="display: none;">
<td>
</td>
<td style="padding-left: 2em;">
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>If "divided from" is anything to go by, this quatrain may have been written in David's new territories in southern Scotland.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> The lands in question consisted of the <a href="/wiki/Pre-1975_counties" class="mw-redirect" title="Pre-1975 counties">pre-1975 counties</a> of <a href="/wiki/Roxburghshire" title="Roxburghshire">Roxburghshire</a>, <a href="/wiki/Selkirkshire" title="Selkirkshire">Selkirkshire</a>, <a href="/wiki/Berwickshire" title="Berwickshire">Berwickshire</a>, <a href="/wiki/Peeblesshire" title="Peeblesshire">Peeblesshire</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lanarkshire" title="Lanarkshire">Lanarkshire</a>. David, moreover, gained the title <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">princeps Cumbrensis</i></span>, "<a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Strathclyde" title="Kingdom of Strathclyde">Prince of the Cumbrians</a>", as attested in David's charters from this era.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Although this was a large slice of Scotland south of the river Forth, the region of Galloway-proper was entirely outside David's control.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> David may perhaps have had varying degrees of overlordship in parts of <a href="/wiki/Dumfriesshire" title="Dumfriesshire">Dumfriesshire</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ayrshire" title="Ayrshire">Ayrshire</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dunbartonshire" title="Dunbartonshire">Dunbartonshire</a> and <a href="/wiki/Renfrewshire_(historic)" title="Renfrewshire (historic)">Renfrewshire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> In the lands between Galloway and the Principality of Cumbria, David eventually set up large-scale marcher lordships, such as <a href="/wiki/Annandale,_Dumfries_and_Galloway" title="Annandale, Dumfries and Galloway">Annandale</a> for <a href="/wiki/Robert_de_Brus,_1st_Lord_of_Annandale" title="Robert de Brus, 1st Lord of Annandale">Robert de Brus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cunninghame" title="Cunninghame">Cunningham</a> for <a href="/wiki/Hugh_de_Morville,_Constable_of_Scotland" title="Hugh de Morville, Constable of Scotland">Hugh de Morville</a>, and possibly <a href="/wiki/Strathgryfe" class="mw-redirect" title="Strathgryfe">Strathgryfe</a> for <a href="/wiki/Walter_Fitzalan" class="mw-redirect" title="Walter Fitzalan">Walter Fitzalan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Earl_of_Huntingdon">Earl of Huntingdon</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Earl of Huntingdon">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Henry1.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Henry1.jpg/180px-Henry1.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Henry1.jpg/270px-Henry1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Henry1.jpg/360px-Henry1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="921" data-file-height="1500" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Henry1.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>King <a href="/wiki/Henry_I_of_England" title="Henry I of England">Henry I of England</a>, drawn by <a href="/wiki/Matthew_Paris" title="Matthew Paris">Matthew Paris</a>. Henry's policy in northern Britain and the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Sea" title="Irish Sea">Irish Sea</a> region essentially made David's political life.</div></div></div>
<p>In the later part of 1113, King Henry gave David the hand of Matilda of Huntingdon, daughter and heiress of <a href="/wiki/Waltheof,_Earl_of_Northumbria" title="Waltheof, Earl of Northumbria">Waltheof, Earl of Northumberland</a>. The marriage brought with it the "Honour of Huntingdon", a lordship scattered through the shires of <a href="/wiki/Northampton" title="Northampton">Northampton</a>, <a href="/wiki/Huntingdon" title="Huntingdon">Huntingdon</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Bedford" title="Bedford">Bedford</a>. Within a few years, Matilda bore a son, whom David named <a href="/wiki/Henry_of_Scotland" title="Henry of Scotland">Henry</a> after his patron.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The new territories which David controlled were a valuable supplement to his income and manpower, increasing his status as one of the most powerful magnates in the Kingdom of the English. Moreover, Matilda's father Waltheof had been <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Northumberland" title="Earl of Northumberland">Earl of Northumberland</a>, a defunct lordship which had covered the far north of England and included <a href="/wiki/Cumberland" title="Cumberland">Cumberland</a> and <a href="/wiki/Westmorland" title="Westmorland">Westmorland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Northumberland" title="Northumberland">Northumberland</a>-proper, as well as overlordship of the bishopric of Durham. After King Henry's death, David revived the claim to this earldom for his son, <a href="/wiki/Henry_of_Scotland" title="Henry of Scotland">Henry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>David's activities and whereabouts after 1114 are not always easy to trace. He spent much of his time outside his principality, in England and in Normandy. Despite the death of his sister on 1 May 1118, David still possessed the favour of King Henry when his brother Alexander died in 1124, leaving Scotland without a king.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Political_and_military_events_in_Scotland_during_David.27s_kingship"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_and_military_events_in_Scotland_during_David's_kingship">Political and military events in Scotland during David's kingship</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Political and military events in Scotland during David&#039;s kingship">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Political_and_military_events_in_Scotland_during_the_reign_of_David_I" title="Political and military events in Scotland during the reign of David I">Political and military events in Scotland during the reign of David I</a></div>
<p>In spite of the fact that King David spent his childhood in Scotland, Michael Lynch and Richard Oram portray David as having little initial connection with the culture and society of the Scots;<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> but both likewise argue that David became increasingly re-Gaelicised in the later stages of his reign.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Whatever the case, David's claim to be heir to the Scottish kingdom was doubtful. However, the Scots never followed the Norman laws of primogeniture. David was the youngest of eight sons of the fifth from last king. Two more recent kings had produced sons, <a href="/wiki/William_fitz_Duncan" title="William fitz Duncan">William fitz Duncan</a>, son of King Donnchad II, and <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A1el_Coluim_mac_Alaxandair" title="Máel Coluim mac Alaxandair">Máel Coluim</a>, son of the last king Alexander, but since Scots had never adopted the rules of  <a href="/wiki/Primogeniture" title="Primogeniture">primogeniture</a> that was not a barrier to his kingship, and unlike David, neither William nor Máel Coluim had the support of Henry. So when Alexander died in 1124, the aristocracy of Scotland could either accept David as king, or face war with both David and Henry I.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Coronation_and_struggle_for_the_kingdom">Coronation and struggle for the kingdom</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Coronation and struggle for the kingdom">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Alexander's son, Máel Coluim, chose war. <a href="/wiki/Orderic_Vitalis" title="Orderic Vitalis">Orderic Vitalis</a> reported that Máel Coluim mac Alaxandair "affected to snatch the kingdom from [David], and fought against him two sufficiently fierce battles; but David, who was loftier in understanding and in power and wealth, conquered him and his followers".<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Máel Coluim escaped unharmed into areas of Scotland not yet under David's control, and in those areas gained shelter and aid.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In either April or May of the same year, David was crowned King of Scotland (<a href="/wiki/Old_Irish_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Irish language">Old Irish</a>: <i lang="sga"><a href="/wiki/R%C3%AD" title="Rí">rí</a>(gh) Alban</i>; <a href="/wiki/Medieval_Latin" title="Medieval Latin">Medieval Latin</a>: <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la"><a href="/wiki/Rex_Scotorum" class="mw-redirect" title="Rex Scotorum">rex Scottorum</a></i></span>)<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> at <a href="/wiki/Scone,_Scotland" title="Scone, Scotland">Scone</a>. If later Scottish and Irish evidence can be taken as evidence, the ceremony of coronation was a series of elaborate traditional rituals,<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> of the kind infamous in the Anglo-French world of the 12th century for their "unchristian" elements.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Ailred of Rievaulx, friend and one-time member of David's court, reported that David "so abhorred those acts of homage which are offered by the Scottish nation in the manner of their fathers upon the recent promotion of their kings, that he was with difficulty compelled by the bishops to receive them".<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Outside his Cumbrian principality and the southern fringe of Scotland-proper, David exercised little power in the 1120s, and in the words of Richard Oram, was "king of Scots in little more than name".<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> He was probably in that part of Scotland he did rule for most of the time between late 1127 and 1130.<sup id="cite_ref-Oram_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Oram-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> However, he was at the court of Henry in 1126 and in early 1127,<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> and returned to Henry's court in 1130, serving as a judge at <a href="/wiki/Woodstock_Palace" title="Woodstock Palace">Woodstock</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Treason" title="Treason">treason</a> trial of <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_de_Clinton" title="Geoffrey de Clinton">Geoffrey de Clinton</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Oram_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Oram-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> It was in this year that David's wife, Matilda of Huntingdon, died. Possibly as a result of this,<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> and while David was still in southern England,<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Scotland-proper rose up in arms against him. The instigator was, again, his nephew Máel Coluim, who now had the support of <a href="/wiki/%C3%93engus_of_Moray" title="Óengus of Moray">Óengus of Moray</a>. King Óengus was David's most powerful vassal, a man who, as grandson of King <a href="/wiki/Lulach_of_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="Lulach of Scotland">Lulach of Scotland</a>, even had his own claim to the kingdom. The rebel Scots had advanced into <a href="/wiki/Angus,_Scotland" title="Angus, Scotland">Angus</a>, where they were met by David's <a href="/wiki/Mercia" title="Mercia">Mercian</a> <a href="/wiki/Constable" title="Constable">constable</a>, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Edward_the_Constable&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Edward the Constable (page does not exist)">Edward</a>; a battle took place at <a href="/wiki/Stracathro" title="Stracathro">Stracathro</a> near <a href="/wiki/Brechin" title="Brechin">Brechin</a>. According to the <i><a href="/wiki/Annals_of_Ulster" title="Annals of Ulster">Annals of Ulster</a></i>, 1000 of Edward's army, and 4000 of Óengus' army – including Óengus himself – died.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Orderic Vitalis, Edward followed up the killing of Óengus by marching north into Moray itself, which, in Orderic's words, "lacked a defender and lord"; and so Edward, "with God's help obtained the entire duchy of that extensive district".<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> However, this was far from the end of it. Máel Coluim escaped, and four years of continuing civil war followed; for David this period was quite simply a "struggle for survival".<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It appears that David asked for and obtained extensive military aid from King Henry. Ailred of Rievaulx related that at this point a large fleet and a large army of Norman knights, including Walter l'Espec, were sent by Henry to Carlisle in order to assist David's attempt to root out his Scottish enemies.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> The fleet seems to have been used in the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Sea" title="Irish Sea">Irish Sea</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Firth_of_Clyde" title="Firth of Clyde">Firth of Clyde</a> and the entire <a href="/wiki/Argyll" title="Argyll">Argyll</a> coast, where Máel Coluim was probably at large among supporters. In 1134, Máel Coluim was captured and imprisoned in <a href="/wiki/Roxburgh_Castle" title="Roxburgh Castle">Roxburgh Castle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Since modern historians no longer confuse him with "<a href="/wiki/Malcolm_MacHeth" class="mw-redirect" title="Malcolm MacHeth">Malcolm MacHeth</a>", it is clear that nothing more is ever heard of Máel Coluim mac Alaxandair, except perhaps that his sons were later allied with <a href="/wiki/Somerled" title="Somerled">Somerled</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Pacification_of_the_west_and_north">Pacification of the west and north</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Pacification of the west and north">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Richard Oram puts forward the suggestion that it was during this period that David granted Walter fitz Alan <a href="/wiki/Strathgryfe" class="mw-redirect" title="Strathgryfe">Strathgryfe</a>, with northern <a href="/wiki/Kyle,_Ayrshire" title="Kyle, Ayrshire">Kyle</a> and the area around <a href="/wiki/Renfrew,_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="Renfrew, Scotland">Renfrew</a>, forming what would become the "Stewart" lordship of Strathgryfe; he also suggests that Hugh de Morville may have gained <a href="/wiki/Cunninghame" title="Cunninghame">Cunningham</a> and the settlement of "Strathyrewen" (i.e. <a href="/wiki/Irvine,_North_Ayrshire" title="Irvine, North Ayrshire">Irvine</a>). This would indicate that the 1130–34 campaign had resulted in the acquisition of these territories.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>How long it took to pacify Moray is not known, but in this period David appointed his nephew William fitz Duncan to succeed Óengus, perhaps in compensation for the exclusion from the succession to the Scottish throne caused by the coming of age of David's son <a href="/wiki/Henry_of_Scotland" title="Henry of Scotland">Henry</a>. William may have been given the daughter of Óengus in marriage, cementing his authority in the region. The burghs of <a href="/wiki/Elgin,_Moray" title="Elgin, Moray">Elgin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Forres" title="Forres">Forres</a> may have been founded at this point, consolidating royal authority in Moray.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> David also founded <a href="/wiki/Urquhart_Priory" title="Urquhart Priory">Urquhart Priory</a>, possibly as a "victory monastery", and assigned to it a percentage of his <i><a href="/wiki/Cain" title="Cain">cain</a></i> (tribute) from Argyll.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During this period too, a marriage was arranged between the son of <a href="/wiki/Matad,_Mormaer_of_Atholl" class="mw-redirect" title="Matad, Mormaer of Atholl">Matad, Mormaer of Atholl</a>, and the daughter of <a href="/wiki/Haakon_Paulsson" title="Haakon Paulsson">Haakon Paulsson</a>, <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Orkney" title="Earl of Orkney">Earl of Orkney</a>. The marriage temporarily secured the northern frontier of the Kingdom, and held out the prospect that a son of one of David's <a href="/wiki/Mormaer" title="Mormaer">mormaers</a> could gain <a href="/wiki/Orkney" title="Orkney">Orkney</a> and <a href="/wiki/Caithness" title="Caithness">Caithness</a> for the Kingdom of Scotland. Thus, by the time Henry I died on 1 December 1135, David had more of Scotland under his control than ever before.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dominating_the_north">Dominating the north</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Dominating the north">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Kinloss_Abbey.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Kinloss_Abbey.jpg/220px-Kinloss_Abbey.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="159" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Kinloss_Abbey.jpg/330px-Kinloss_Abbey.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Kinloss_Abbey.jpg/440px-Kinloss_Abbey.jpg 2x" data-file-width="553" data-file-height="400" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Kinloss_Abbey.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The ruins of <a href="/wiki/Kinloss_Abbey" title="Kinloss Abbey">Kinloss Abbey</a> in Moray, founded by David in 1150 for a colony of Melrose Cistercians.</div></div></div>
<p>While fighting <a href="/wiki/Stephen_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Stephen of England">King Stephen</a> and attempting to dominate northern England in the years following 1136, David was continuing his drive for control of the far north of Scotland. In 1139, his cousin, the five-year-old <a href="/wiki/Harald_Maddadsson" title="Harald Maddadsson">Harald Maddadsson</a>, was given the title of "Earl" and half the lands of the <a href="/wiki/Earldom_of_Orkney" title="Earldom of Orkney">earldom of Orkney</a>, in addition to Scottish Caithness. Throughout the 1140s Caithness and Sutherland were brought back under the Scottish zone of control.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Sometime before 1146 David appointed a native Scot called <a href="/wiki/Aindr%C3%A9as_of_Caithness" title="Aindréas of Caithness">Aindréas</a> to be the first <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Caithness" title="Bishop of Caithness">Bishop of Caithness</a>, a bishopric which was based at <a href="/wiki/Halkirk" title="Halkirk">Halkirk</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Thurso" title="Thurso">Thurso</a>, in an area which was ethnically Scandinavian.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1150, it looked like Caithness and the whole earldom of Orkney were going to come under permanent Scottish control. However, David's plans for the north soon began to encounter problems. In 1151, King <a href="/wiki/Eystein_II_of_Norway" class="mw-redirect" title="Eystein II of Norway">Eystein II of Norway</a> put a spanner in the works by sailing through the waterways of Orkney with a large fleet and catching the young Harald unaware in his residence at Thurso. Eystein forced Harald to pay <a href="/wiki/Fealty" title="Fealty">fealty</a> as a condition of his release. Later in the year David hastily responded by supporting the claims to the Orkney earldom of Harald's rival <a href="/wiki/Erlend_Haraldsson" title="Erlend Haraldsson">Erlend Haraldsson</a>, granting him half of Caithness in opposition to Harald. King Eystein responded in turn by making a similar grant to this same Erlend, cancelling the effect of David's grant. David's weakness in Orkney was that the Norwegian kings were not prepared to stand back and let him reduce their power.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="England">England</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: England">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Stepan_Blois.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Stepan_Blois.jpg/170px-Stepan_Blois.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="268" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Stepan_Blois.jpg/255px-Stepan_Blois.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/23/Stepan_Blois.jpg 2x" data-file-width="284" data-file-height="448" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Stepan_Blois.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>King Stephen drawn by Matthew Paris. David used Stephen's "<a href="/wiki/Usurpation" class="mw-redirect" title="Usurpation">usurpation</a>" as his <i><a href="/wiki/Casus_belli" title="Casus belli">casus belli</a></i> with England, even if it was not the actual reason.</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/England_and_King_David_I" title="England and King David I">England and King David I</a></div>
<p>David's relationship with England and the English crown in these years is usually interpreted in two ways. Firstly, his actions are understood in relation to his connections with the King of England. No historian is likely to deny that David's early career was largely manufactured by King Henry I of England. David was the latter's brother-in-law and "greatest protégé",<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> one of Henry's "new men".<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> His hostility to Stephen can be interpreted as an effort to uphold the intended inheritance of Henry I, the succession of his daughter and David's niece <a href="/wiki/Empress_Matilda" title="Empress Matilda">Empress Matilda</a>. David carried out his wars in her name, joined her when she arrived in England, and later knighted her son <a href="/wiki/Henry_II_of_England" title="Henry II of England">Henry</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, David's policy towards England can be interpreted in an additional way. David was the independence-loving king trying to build a "Scoto-Northumbrian" realm by seizing the most northerly parts of the English kingdom. In this perspective, David's support for Matilda is used as a pretext for land-grabbing. David's maternal descent from the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Wessex" title="House of Wessex">House of Wessex</a> and his son Henry's maternal descent from the English earls of Northumberland is thought to have further encouraged such a project, a project which came to an end only after Henry II ordered David's child successor <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A1el_Coluim_IV" class="mw-redirect" title="Máel Coluim IV">Máel Coluim IV</a> to hand over the most important of David's gains. It is clear that neither one of these interpretations can be taken without some weight being given to the other.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Usurpation_of_Stephen_and_First_Treaty_of_Durham">Usurpation of Stephen and First Treaty of Durham</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Usurpation of Stephen and First Treaty of Durham">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Henry I had arranged his inheritance to pass to his daughter <a href="/wiki/Empress_Matilda" title="Empress Matilda">Empress Matilda</a>. Instead, <a href="/wiki/Stephen_of_England" class="mw-redirect" title="Stephen of England">Stephen</a>, younger brother of <a href="/wiki/Theobald_II,_Count_of_Blois" class="mw-redirect" title="Theobald II, Count of Blois">Theobald II, Count of Blois</a>, seized the throne.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> David had been the first lay person to take the oath to uphold the succession of Matilda in 1127, and when Stephen was crowned on 22 December 1135, David decided to make war.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Before December was over, David marched into northern England, and by the end of January he had occupied the castles of <a href="/wiki/Carlisle_Castle" title="Carlisle Castle">Carlisle</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wark_on_Tyne" title="Wark on Tyne">Wark</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alnwick" title="Alnwick">Alnwick</a>, <a href="/wiki/Norham" title="Norham">Norham</a> and <a href="/wiki/Newcastle_upon_Tyne" title="Newcastle upon Tyne">Newcastle</a>. By February David was at Durham, but an army led by King Stephen met him there. Rather than fight a pitched battle, a treaty was agreed whereby David would retain Carlisle, while David's son Henry was re-granted the title and half the lands of the earldom of Huntingdon, territory which had been confiscated during David's revolt. On Stephen's side he received back the other castles; and while David would do no homage, Stephen was to receive the homage of Henry for both Carlisle and the other English territories. Stephen also gave the rather worthless but for David face-saving promise that if he ever chose to resurrect the defunct earldom of Northumberland, Henry would be given first consideration. Importantly, the issue of Matilda was not mentioned. However, the first Durham treaty quickly broke down after David took insult at the treatment of his son Henry at Stephen's court.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Renewal_of_war_and_Clitheroe">Renewal of war and Clitheroe</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Renewal of war and Clitheroe">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>When the winter of 1136–37 was over, David prepared again to invade England. The King of the Scots massed an army on Northumberland's border, to which the English responded by gathering an army at Newcastle.<sup id="cite_ref-Crouch_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Crouch-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> Once more pitched battle was avoided, and instead a truce was agreed until December.<sup id="cite_ref-Crouch_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Crouch-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> When December fell, David demanded that Stephen hand over the whole of the old earldom of Northumberland. Stephen's refusal led to David's third invasion, this time in January 1138.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The army which invaded England in January and February 1138 shocked the English chroniclers. <a href="/wiki/Richard_of_Hexham" title="Richard of Hexham">Richard of Hexham</a> called it "an execrable army, savager than any race of heathen yielding honour to neither God nor man" and that it "harried the whole province and slaughtered everywhere folk of either sex, of every age and condition, destroying, pillaging and burning the vills, churches and houses".<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Several doubtful stories of cannibalism were recorded by chroniclers, and these same chroniclers paint a picture of routine enslavings, as well as killings of churchmen, women and infants.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By February King Stephen marched north to deal with David. The two armies avoided each other, and Stephen was soon on the road south. In the summer David split his army into two forces, sending William fitz Duncan to march into <a href="/wiki/Lancashire" title="Lancashire">Lancashire</a>, where he harried <a href="/wiki/Furness" title="Furness">Furness</a> and <a href="/wiki/Craven_in_the_Domesday_Book" title="Craven in the Domesday Book">Craven</a>. On 10 June, William fitz Duncan met a force of knights and men-at-arms. A pitched battle took place, the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Clitheroe" title="Battle of Clitheroe">battle of Clitheroe</a>, and the English army was routed.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Battle_of_the_Standard_and_Second_Treaty_of_Durham">Battle of the Standard and Second Treaty of Durham</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Battle of the Standard and Second Treaty of Durham">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>By later July 1138, the two Scottish armies had reunited in "St Cuthbert's land", that is, in the lands controlled by the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Durham" title="Bishop of Durham">Bishop of Durham</a>, on the far side of the <a href="/wiki/River_Tyne" title="River Tyne">river Tyne</a>. Another English army had mustered to meet the Scots, this time led by <a href="/wiki/William,_Earl_of_Aumale" class="mw-redirect" title="William, Earl of Aumale">William, Earl of Aumale</a>. The victory at Clitheroe was probably what inspired David to risk battle. David's force, apparently 26,000 strong and several times larger than the English army, met the English on 22 August at Cowdon Moor near <a href="/wiki/Northallerton" title="Northallerton">Northallerton</a>, <a href="/wiki/North_Yorkshire" title="North Yorkshire">North Yorkshire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Seal_(rev).JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/David_Seal_%28rev%29.JPG/220px-David_Seal_%28rev%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="222" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/David_Seal_%28rev%29.JPG/330px-David_Seal_%28rev%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/David_Seal_%28rev%29.JPG/440px-David_Seal_%28rev%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="831" data-file-height="838" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Seal_(rev).JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Steel engraving and enhancement of the reverse side of the Great Seal of David I, a picture in the Anglo-Continental style depicting David as a warrior leader.</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Standard" title="Battle of the Standard">Battle of the Standard</a>, as the encounter came to be called, was a defeat for the Scots. Afterwards, David and his surviving notables retired to Carlisle. Although the result was a defeat, it was not by any means decisive. David retained the bulk of his army and thus the power to go on the offensive again. The <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Wark_(1138)" title="Siege of Wark (1138)">siege of Wark</a>, for instance, which had been going on since January, continued until it was captured in November. David continued to occupy <a href="/wiki/Cumberland" title="Cumberland">Cumberland</a> as well as much of <a href="/wiki/Northumberland" title="Northumberland">Northumberland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Oram_2_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Oram_2-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 26 September <a href="/wiki/Alberic_of_Ostia" title="Alberic of Ostia">Cardinal Alberic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Ostia" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Ostia">Bishop of Ostia</a>, arrived at Carlisle where David had called together his kingdom's nobles, abbots and bishops. Alberic was there to investigate the controversy over the issue of the Bishop of Glasgow's allegiance or non-allegiance to the Archbishop of York. Alberic played the role of peace-broker, and David agreed to a six-week truce which excluded the siege of Wark. On 9 April David and Stephen's wife <a href="/wiki/Matilda_of_Boulogne" class="mw-redirect" title="Matilda of Boulogne">Matilda of Boulogne</a> (daughter of <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Scotland,_Countess_of_Boulogne" title="Mary of Scotland, Countess of Boulogne">Mary of Scotland</a>, and so another niece of David) met each other at Durham and agreed a settlement. David's son Henry was given the earldom of Northumberland and was restored to the earldom of Huntingdon and lordship of <a href="/wiki/Doncaster" title="Doncaster">Doncaster</a>; David himself was allowed to keep Carlisle and Cumberland. King Stephen was to retain possession of the strategically vital castles of <a href="/wiki/Bamburgh" title="Bamburgh">Bamburgh</a> and Newcastle. This effectively fulfilled all of David's war aims.<sup id="cite_ref-Oram_2_71-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Oram_2-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Arrival_of_Matilda_and_the_renewal_of_conflict">Arrival of Matilda and the renewal of conflict</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Arrival of Matilda and the renewal of conflict">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The settlement with Stephen was not set to last long. The arrival in England of the Empress Matilda gave David an opportunity to renew the conflict with Stephen. In either May or June, David travelled to the south of England and entered Matilda's company; he was present for her expected coronation at <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Abbey" title="Westminster Abbey">Westminster Abbey</a>, though this never took place. David was there until September, when the Empress found herself surrounded at <a href="/wiki/Rout_of_Winchester" title="Rout of Winchester">Winchester</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This civil war, or "<a href="/wiki/The_Anarchy" title="The Anarchy">the Anarchy</a>" as it was later called, enabled David to strengthen his own position in northern England. While David consolidated his hold on his own and his son's newly acquired lands, he also sought to expand his influence. The castles at Newcastle and Bamburgh were again brought under his control, and he attained dominion over all of England north-west of the <a href="/wiki/River_Ribble" title="River Ribble">river Ribble</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pennines" title="Pennines">Pennines</a>, while holding the north-east as far south as the river Tyne, on the borders of the core territory of the bishopric of Durham. While his son brought all the senior barons of Northumberland into his entourage, David rebuilt the fortress of Carlisle. Carlisle quickly replaced Roxburgh as his favoured residence. David's acquisition of the mines at <a href="/wiki/Alston,_Cumbria" title="Alston, Cumbria">Alston</a> on the <a href="/wiki/River_Tyne" title="River Tyne">South Tyne</a> enabled him to begin minting the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Scotland" title="Kingdom of Scotland">Kingdom of Scotland</a>'s first silver coinage. David, meanwhile, issued charters to <a href="/wiki/Shrewsbury_Abbey" title="Shrewsbury Abbey">Shrewsbury Abbey</a> in respect to their lands in <a href="/wiki/Lancashire" title="Lancashire">Lancashire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Bishopric_of_Durham_and_the_Archbishopric_of_York">Bishopric of Durham and the Archbishopric of York</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Bishopric of Durham and the Archbishopric of York">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>However, David's successes were in many ways balanced by his failures. David's greatest disappointment during this time was his inability to ensure control of the bishopric of Durham and the archbishopric of York. David had attempted to appoint his chancellor, William Comyn, to the bishopric of Durham, which had been vacant since the death of Bishop <a href="/wiki/Geoffrey_Rufus" title="Geoffrey Rufus">Geoffrey Rufus</a> in 1140. Between 1141 and 1143, Comyn was the <i>de facto</i> bishop, and had control of the bishop's castle; but he was resented by the <a href="/wiki/Chapter_(religion)" title="Chapter (religion)">chapter</a>. Despite controlling the town of Durham, David's only hope of ensuring his election and consecration was gaining the support of the Papal legate, <a href="/wiki/Henry_of_Blois" title="Henry of Blois">Henry of Blois</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Winchester" title="Bishop of Winchester">Bishop of Winchester</a> and brother of King Stephen. Despite obtaining the support of the Empress Matilda, David was unsuccessful and had given up by the time <a href="/wiki/William_de_St_Barbara" class="mw-redirect" title="William de St Barbara">William de St Barbara</a> was elected to the see in 1143.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>David also attempted to interfere in the succession to the archbishopric of York. <a href="/wiki/Saint_William_of_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint William of York">William FitzHerbert</a>, nephew of King Stephen, found his position undermined by the collapsing political fortune of Stephen in the north of England, and was deposed by the Pope. David used his Cistercian connections to build a bond with <a href="/wiki/Henry_Murdac" title="Henry Murdac">Henry Murdac</a>, the new archbishop. Despite the support of <a href="/wiki/Pope_Eugenius_III" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Eugenius III">Pope Eugenius III</a>, supporters of King Stephen and William FitzHerbert managed to prevent Henry taking up his post at York. In 1149, Henry had sought the support of David. David seized on the opportunity to bring the archdiocese under his control, and marched on the city. However, Stephen's supporters became aware of David's intentions, and informed King Stephen. Stephen therefore marched to the city and installed a new garrison. David decided not to risk such an engagement and withdrew.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Richard Oram has conjectured that David's ultimate aim was to bring the whole of the ancient <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Northumbria" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of Northumbria">kingdom of Northumbria</a> into his dominion. For Oram, this event was the turning point, "the chance to radically redraw the political map of the British Isles lost forever".<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Scottish_Church">Scottish Church</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Scottish Church">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/David_I_and_the_Scottish_Church" title="David I and the Scottish Church">David I and the Scottish Church</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Seal_(ob).JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/David_Seal_%28ob%29.JPG/220px-David_Seal_%28ob%29.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="223" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/David_Seal_%28ob%29.JPG/330px-David_Seal_%28ob%29.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/David_Seal_%28ob%29.JPG/440px-David_Seal_%28ob%29.JPG 2x" data-file-width="861" data-file-height="873" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:David_Seal_(ob).JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Steel engraving and enhancement of the obverse side of the Great Seal of David I, portraying David in the "Continental" fashion the other-worldly maintainer of peace and defender of justice.</div></div></div>
<p><span class="citation-needed-content" style="padding-left:0.1em; padding-right:0.1em; color:#595959; border:1px solid #DDD;">Historical treatment of David I and the Scottish church usually emphasises David's pioneering role as the instrument of diocesan reorganisation and Norman penetration, beginning with the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Glasgow" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Glasgow">bishopric of Glasgow</a> while David was Prince of the Cumbrians, and continuing further north after David acceded to the throne of Scotland. Focus too is usually given to his role as the defender of the Scottish church's independence from claims of overlordship by the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_York" title="Archbishop of York">Archbishop of York</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Canterbury" title="Archbishop of Canterbury">Archbishop of Canterbury</a>.</span><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Innovations_in_the_church_system">Innovations in the church system</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Innovations in the church system">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>It was once held that Scotland's episcopal sees and entire parochial system owed its origins to the innovations of David I. Today, scholars have moderated this view. <a href="/wiki/Ailred_of_Rievaulx" class="mw-redirect" title="Ailred of Rievaulx">Ailred of Rievaulx</a> wrote in David's eulogy that when David came to power, "he found three or four bishops in the whole Scottish kingdom [north of the Forth], and the others wavering without a pastor to the loss of both morals and property; when he died, he left nine, both of ancient bishoprics which he himself restored, and new ones which he erected".<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Although David moved the bishopric of <a href="/wiki/Mortlach,_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="Mortlach, Scotland">Mortlach</a> east to his new burgh of Aberdeen, and arranged the creation of the diocese of Caithness, no other bishoprics can be safely called David's creation.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Glasgow" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Glasgow">bishopric of Glasgow</a> was restored rather than resurrected.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> David appointed his reform-minded French chaplain <a href="/wiki/John_Capellanus" title="John Capellanus">John</a> to the bishopric<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> and carried out an <a href="/wiki/Inquest" title="Inquest">inquest</a>, afterwards assigning to the bishopric all the lands of his principality, except those in the east which were already governed by the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_St_Andrews" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of St Andrews">Bishop of St Andrews</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> David was at least partly responsible for forcing semi-monastic "bishoprics" like <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Brechin" title="Bishop of Brechin">Brechin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Dunkeld" title="Bishop of Dunkeld">Dunkeld</a>, Mortlach (Aberdeen) and <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Dunblane" title="Bishop of Dunblane">Dunblane</a> to become fully episcopal and firmly integrated into a national diocesan system.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As for the development of the parochial system, David's traditional role as its creator can not be sustained.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Scotland already had an ancient system of parish churches dating to the <a href="/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages" title="Early Middle Ages">Early Middle Ages</a>, and the kind of system introduced by David's Normanising tendencies can more accurately be seen as mild refashioning, rather than creation; he made the Scottish system as a whole more like that of France and England, but he did not create it.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ecclesiastical_disputes">Ecclesiastical disputes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Ecclesiastical disputes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>One of the first problems David had to deal with as king was an ecclesiastical dispute with the English church. The problem with the English church concerned the subordination of Scottish sees to the archbishops of York and/or Canterbury, an issue which since his election in 1124 had prevented <a href="/wiki/Robert_of_Scone" title="Robert of Scone">Robert of Scone</a> from being consecrated to the see of <a href="/wiki/St_Andrews" title="St Andrews">St Andrews</a> (Cell Ríghmonaidh). It is likely that since the 11th century the bishopric of St Andrews functioned as a <i>de facto</i> archbishopric. The title of "Archbishop" is accorded in Scottish and Irish sources to <a href="/wiki/Giric_of_Cennr%C3%ADgmonaid" class="mw-redirect" title="Giric of Cennrígmonaid">Bishop Giric</a><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Fothad_II_of_Cennr%C3%ADgmonaid" class="mw-redirect" title="Fothad II of Cennrígmonaid">Bishop Fothad II</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Rules_Tower.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/St_Rules_Tower.jpg/220px-St_Rules_Tower.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/St_Rules_Tower.jpg/330px-St_Rules_Tower.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/St_Rules_Tower.jpg/440px-St_Rules_Tower.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="800" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Rules_Tower.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The tower of the church of St Riagal (<a href="/wiki/Saint_Regulus" title="Saint Regulus">Saint Regulus</a>), at Cenn Ríghmonaidh, later named (<a href="/wiki/St_Andrews" title="St Andrews">St Andrews</a>); this existed during David's reign.</div></div></div>
<p>The problem was that this archiepiscopal status had not been cleared with the papacy, opening the way for English archbishops to claim overlordship of the whole Scottish church. The man responsible was the new aggressively assertive Archbishop of York, <a href="/wiki/Thurstan" title="Thurstan">Thurstan</a>. His easiest target was the bishopric of Glasgow, which being south of the <a href="/wiki/River_Forth" title="River Forth">river Forth</a> was not regarded as part of Scotland nor the jurisdiction of St Andrews. In 1125, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Honorius_II" title="Pope Honorius II">Pope Honorius II</a> wrote to John, Bishop of Glasgow ordering him to submit to the archbishopric of York.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> David ordered Bishop John of Glasgow to travel to the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Apostolic See</a> in order to secure a <a href="/wiki/Pallium" title="Pallium">pallium</a> which would elevate the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_St_Andrews" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of St Andrews">bishopric of St Andrews</a> to an archbishopric with jurisdiction over Glasgow.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thurstan travelled to Rome, as did the Archbishop of Canterbury, <a href="/wiki/William_de_Corbeil" title="William de Corbeil">William de Corbeil</a>, and both presumably opposed David's request. David however gained the support of King Henry, and the Archbishop of York agreed to a year's postponement of the issue and to consecrate Robert of Scone without making an issue of subordination.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> York's claim over bishops north of the Forth were in practice abandoned for the rest of David's reign, although York maintained her more credible claims over Glasgow.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1151, David again requested a pallium for the Archbishop of St Andrews. Cardinal John Paparo met David at his residence of Carlisle in September 1151. Tantalisingly for David, the Cardinal was on his way to Ireland with four <i>pallia</i> to create four new Irish archbishoprics. When the Cardinal returned to Carlisle, David made the request. In David's plan, the new archdiocese would include all the bishoprics in David's Scottish territory, as well as <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Orkney" title="Bishop of Orkney">bishopric of Orkney</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_the_Isles" title="Bishop of the Isles">bishopric of the Isles</a>. Unfortunately for David, the Cardinal does not appear to have brought the issue up with the papacy. In the following year the papacy dealt David another blow by creating the archbishopric of Trondheim, a new Norwegian archbishopric embracing the bishoprics of the Isles and Orkney.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Succession_and_death">Succession and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Succession and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:David_I_and_Malcolm_IV.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/David_I_and_Malcolm_IV.jpg/200px-David_I_and_Malcolm_IV.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="153" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/David_I_and_Malcolm_IV.jpg/300px-David_I_and_Malcolm_IV.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/68/David_I_and_Malcolm_IV.jpg 2x" data-file-width="330" data-file-height="253" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:David_I_and_Malcolm_IV.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>David alongside his designated successor, <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A1el_Coluim_mac_Eanric" class="mw-redirect" title="Máel Coluim mac Eanric">Máel Coluim mac Eanric</a>. Máel Coluim IV would reign for twelve years, in a reign marked for the young king's chastity and religious fervour.</div></div></div>
<p>Perhaps the greatest blow to David's plans came on 12 July 1152 when Henry, Earl of Northumberland, David's only son and heir, died. He had probably been suffering from some kind of illness for a long time. David had under a year to live, and he may have known that he was not going to be alive much longer. David quickly arranged for his grandson <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_IV" class="mw-redirect" title="Malcolm IV">Malcolm IV</a> to be made his successor, and for his younger grandson <a href="/wiki/William_I_of_Scotland" class="mw-redirect" title="William I of Scotland">William</a> to be made Earl of Northumberland. <a href="/wiki/Donnchad_I,_Mormaer_of_Fife" class="mw-redirect" title="Donnchad I, Mormaer of Fife">Donnchad I, Mormaer of Fife</a>, the senior magnate in Scotland-proper, was appointed as <i>rector</i>, or <a href="/wiki/Regent" title="Regent">regent</a>, and took the 11 year-old Malcolm around Scotland-proper on a tour to meet and gain the homage of his future Gaelic subjects. David's health began to fail seriously in the spring of 1153, and on 24 May 1153, David died in <a href="/wiki/Carlisle_Castle" title="Carlisle Castle">Carlisle Castle</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In his obituary in the <i><a href="/wiki/Annals_of_Tigernach" title="Annals of Tigernach">Annals of Tigernach</a></i>, he is called <i>Dabíd mac Mail Colaim, rí Alban &amp; Saxan</i>, "David, son of Malcolm, King of Scotland and England", a title which acknowledged the importance of the new English part of David's realm.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> <span class="citation-needed-content" style="padding-left:0.1em; padding-right:0.1em; color:#595959; border:1px solid #DDD;">He was buried in <a href="/wiki/Dunfermline_Abbey" title="Dunfermline Abbey">Dunfermline Abbey</a>.</span><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>David I is recognised as a saint by the Roman Catholic Church, with a feast day of 24 May, though it appears that he was never formally canonized. There are churches in Scotland which have him as their patron.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> <span class="citation-needed-content" style="padding-left:0.1em; padding-right:0.1em; color:#595959; border:1px solid #DDD;">His mother <a href="/wiki/Saint_Margaret_of_Scotland" title="Saint Margaret of Scotland">Saint Margaret of Scotland</a> was canonised in 1250.</span><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Historiography">Historiography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Historiography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Medieval_reputation">Medieval reputation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Medieval reputation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The earliest English assessments of David I portray him as a pious king, a reformer and a civilising agent in a barbarian nation. For William of Newburgh, David was a "King not barbarous of a barbarous nation", who "wisely tempered the fierceness of his barbarous nation". William praises David for his piety, noting that, among other saintly activities, "he was frequent in washing the feet of the poor" (this can be read literally: his mother, who is now patron saint of Scotland, was widely known and lauded for the same practice).<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Another of David's eulogists, his former courtier <a href="/wiki/Ailred_of_Rievaulx" class="mw-redirect" title="Ailred of Rievaulx">Ailred of Rievaulx</a>, echoes Newburgh's assertions and praises David for his justice as well as his piety, commenting that David's rule of the Scots meant that "the whole barbarity of that nation was softened&#160;... as if forgetting their natural fierceness they submitted their necks to the laws which the royal gentleness dictated".<sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Although avoiding stress on 12th-century Scottish "barbarity", the Lowland Scottish historians of the later Middle Ages tend to repeat the accounts of earlier chronicle tradition. Much that was written was either directly transcribed from the earlier medieval chronicles themselves or was modelled closely upon them, even in the significant works of <a href="/wiki/John_of_Fordun" title="John of Fordun">John of Fordun</a>, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Wyntoun" class="mw-redirect" title="Andrew Wyntoun">Andrew Wyntoun</a> and <a href="/wiki/Walter_Bower" title="Walter Bower">Walter Bower</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> For example, Bower includes in his text the eulogy written for David by Ailred of Rievaulx. This quotation extends to over twenty pages in the modern edition, and exerted a great deal of influence over what became the traditional view of David in later works about Scottish history.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Historical treatment of David developed in the writings of later Scottish historians, and the writings of men like <a href="/wiki/John_Major_(philosopher)" title="John Major (philosopher)">John Mair</a>, <a href="/wiki/George_Buchanan" title="George Buchanan">George Buchanan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hector_Boece" title="Hector Boece">Hector Boece</a>, and Bishop <a href="/wiki/John_Lesley" title="John Lesley">John Leslie</a> ensured that by the 18th century a picture of David as a pious, justice-loving state-builder and vigorous maintainer of Scottish independence had emerged.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Moreover, Bower stated in his eulogy that David had always an ambition to join a <a href="/wiki/Crusade" class="mw-redirect" title="Crusade">crusade</a>, which was prevented eventually by his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacquarrie199781_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacquarrie199781-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> In addition, Ailred of Rievaulx hinted that David expressed his desire to be part of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Crusade" title="Second Crusade">Second Crusade</a> himself, but he was dissuaded by his subjects.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECowanMackayMacquarrie198318_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECowanMackayMacquarrie198318-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> However, David had already met <a href="/wiki/Hugues_de_Payens" title="Hugues de Payens">Hugues de Payens</a>, the first Grand Master of the <a href="/wiki/Knights_Templar" title="Knights Templar">Knights Templar</a>, in 1128 in Scotland.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> <span class="citation-needed-content" style="padding-left:0.1em; padding-right:0.1em; color:#595959; border:1px solid #DDD;">In the meantime, the <a href="/wiki/Knights_Templar_in_Scotland" title="Knights Templar in Scotland">Order</a> established a seat at <a href="/wiki/Balantrodoch" class="mw-redirect" title="Balantrodoch">Balantrodoch</a>, now <a href="/wiki/Temple,_Midlothian" title="Temple, Midlothian">Temple, Midlothian</a> on the South Esk (<a href="/wiki/River_Esk,_Lothian" title="River Esk, Lothian">River Esk, Lothian</a>).</span><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Modern_treatment">Modern treatment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Modern treatment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
