<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:182px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Statue_of_David_I_on_the_West_Door_of_St._Giles_High_Kirk,_Edinburgh.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Statue_of_David_I_on_the_West_Door_of_St._Giles_High_Kirk%2C_Edinburgh.jpg/180px-Statue_of_David_I_on_the_West_Door_of_St._Giles_High_Kirk%2C_Edinburgh.jpg" decoding="async" width="180" height="243" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Statue_of_David_I_on_the_West_Door_of_St._Giles_High_Kirk%2C_Edinburgh.jpg/270px-Statue_of_David_I_on_the_West_Door_of_St._Giles_High_Kirk%2C_Edinburgh.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Statue_of_David_I_on_the_West_Door_of_St._Giles_High_Kirk%2C_Edinburgh.jpg/360px-Statue_of_David_I_on_the_West_Door_of_St._Giles_High_Kirk%2C_Edinburgh.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1889" data-file-height="2553" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Statue_of_David_I_on_the_West_Door_of_St._Giles_High_Kirk,_Edinburgh.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Statue of David I on the West Door of St. Giles High Kirk, Edinburgh</div></div></div>
<p>In the modern period there has been more of an emphasis on David's statebuilding and on the effects of his changes on Scottish cultural development. Lowland Scots tended to trace the origins of their culture to the marriage of David's father Máel Coluim III to <a href="/wiki/Saint_Margaret_of_Scotland" title="Saint Margaret of Scotland">Saint Margaret</a>, a myth which had its origins in the medieval period.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> With the development of modern historical techniques in the mid-19th century, responsibility for these developments appeared to lie more with David than his father. David assumed a principal place in the alleged destruction of the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Alba" title="Kingdom of Alba">Celtic Kingdom of Scotland</a>. Andrew Lang, in 1900, wrote that "with Alexander [I], Celtic domination ends; with David, Norman and English dominance is established".<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The ages of <a href="/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment" title="Age of Enlightenment">Enlightenment</a> and <a href="/wiki/Romanticism" title="Romanticism">Romanticism</a> had elevated the role of races and "ethnic packages" into mainstream history, and in this context David was portrayed as hostile to the native Scots, and his reforms were seen in the light of natural, perhaps even justified, civilised Teutonic aggression towards the backward Celts.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 20th century, several studies were devoted to Normanisation in 12th-century Scotland, focusing upon and hence emphasising the changes brought about by the reign of David I. Græme Ritchie's <i>The Normans in Scotland</i> (1954), <a href="/wiki/Archie_Duncan_(historian)" title="Archie Duncan (historian)">Archie Duncan</a>'s <i>Scotland: The Making of the Kingdom</i> (1974) and the many articles of <a href="/wiki/G._W._S._Barrow" title="G. W. S. Barrow">G. W. S. Barrow</a> all formed part of this historiographical trend.<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 1980s, Barrow sought a compromise between change and continuity, and argued that the reign of King David was in fact a "Balance of New and Old".<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Such a conclusion was a natural incorporation of an underlying current in Scottish historiography which, since <a href="/wiki/William_Forbes_Skene" title="William Forbes Skene">William F. Skene</a>'s monumental and revolutionary three-volume <i>Celtic Scotland: A History of Ancient Alban</i> (1876–80), had been forced to acknowledge that "Celtic Scotland" was alive and healthy for a long time after the reign of David I.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Michael Lynch followed and built upon Barrow's compromise solution, arguing that as David's reign progressed, his kingship became more Celtic.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Despite its subtitle, in 2004 in the only full volume study of David I's reign yet produced, <i>David I: The King Who Made Scotland</i>, its author <a href="/wiki/Richard_Oram" title="Richard Oram">Richard Oram</a> further builds upon Lynch's picture, stressing continuity while placing the changes of David's reign in their context.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Davidian_Revolution">Davidian Revolution</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Davidian Revolution">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Davidian_Revolution" title="Davidian Revolution">Davidian Revolution</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim_Coinage.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/25/Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim_Coinage.JPG/220px-Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim_Coinage.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="383" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/25/Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim_Coinage.JPG 1.5x" data-file-width="221" data-file-height="385" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim_Coinage.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Silver penny of David I.</div></div></div>
<p>However, while there may be debate about the importance or extent of the <a href="/wiki/Historical_change" class="mw-redirect" title="Historical change">historical change</a> in David I's era, no historian doubts that it was taking place. The reason is what Barrow and Lynch both call the "Davidian Revolution".<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> David's "revolution" is held to underpin the development of later medieval Scotland, whereby the changes he inaugurated grew into most of the central institutions of the later medieval kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since <a href="/wiki/Robert_Bartlett_(historian)" title="Robert Bartlett (historian)">Robert Bartlett</a>'s pioneering work, <i>The Making of Europe: Conquest, Colonization and Cultural Change, 950–1350</i> (1993), reinforced by Moore's <i>The First European Revolution, c.970–1215</i> (2000), it has become increasingly apparent that better understanding of David's "revolution" can be achieved by recognising the wider "European revolution" taking place during this period. The central idea is that from the late 10th century onwards the culture and institutions of the old <a href="/wiki/Carolingian" class="mw-redirect" title="Carolingian">Carolingian</a> heartlands in northern <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a> and western <a href="/wiki/Germany" title="Germany">Germany</a> were spreading to outlying areas, creating a more recognisable "Europe". Scotland was just one of many "outlying" areas.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Burghs_of_Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Burghs_of_Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim.JPG/220px-Burghs_of_Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="408" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Burghs_of_Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim.JPG/330px-Burghs_of_Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ac/Burghs_of_Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim.JPG/440px-Burghs_of_Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim.JPG 2x" data-file-width="599" data-file-height="1111" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Burghs_of_Dab%C3%ADd_mac_Ma%C3%ADl_Choluim.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Burghs established in Scotland before the accession of David's successor and grandson, <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_IV_of_Scotland" title="Malcolm IV of Scotland">Máel Coluim IV</a>; these were Scotland's first towns.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This image needs references to reliable sources (December 2022)">image reference needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup></div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Government_and_feudalism">Government and feudalism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Government and feudalism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The widespread enfeoffment of foreign knights and the processes by which <a href="/wiki/Land_ownership" class="mw-redirect" title="Land ownership">land ownership</a> was converted from <a href="/wiki/Custom_(law)" class="mw-redirect" title="Custom (law)">customary</a> <a href="/wiki/Land_tenure" title="Land tenure">tenures</a> into <a href="/wiki/Feudal" class="mw-redirect" title="Feudal">feudal</a>, or otherwise legally-defined relationships, would revolutionise the way the Kingdom of Scotland was governed, as did the dispersal and installation of royal agents in the new <a href="/wiki/Motte-and-bailey" class="mw-redirect" title="Motte-and-bailey">mottes</a> that were proliferating throughout the realm to staff newly created sheriffdoms and judiciaries for the twin purposes of <a href="/wiki/Law_enforcement_organization" class="mw-redirect" title="Law enforcement organization">law enforcement</a> and <a href="/wiki/Taxation" class="mw-redirect" title="Taxation">taxation</a>, bringing Scotland further into the "continental" model.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Scotland in this period experienced innovations in governmental practices and the importation of foreign, mostly <a href="/wiki/French_people" title="French people">French</a>, <a href="/wiki/Knight" title="Knight">knights</a>. It is to David's reign that the beginnings of <a href="/wiki/Feudalism" title="Feudalism">feudalism</a> are generally assigned. This is defined as "castle-building, the regular use of professional cavalry, the knight's fee" as well as "homage and fealty".<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> David established large scale feudal lordships in the west of his Cumbrian principality for the leading members of the French military entourage who kept him in power. Additionally, many smaller scale feudal lordships were created.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Steps were taken during David's reign to make the government of that part of Scotland he administered more like the government of Anglo-Norman England. New <a href="/wiki/Sheriffdom" title="Sheriffdom">sheriffdoms</a> enabled the King to effectively administer <a href="/wiki/Royal_demesne" class="mw-redirect" title="Royal demesne">royal demesne</a> land. During his reign, royal sheriffs were established in the king's core personal territories; namely, in rough chronological order, at <a href="/wiki/Roxburgh" title="Roxburgh">Roxburgh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Scone,_Scotland" title="Scone, Scotland">Scone</a>, <a href="/wiki/Berwick-upon-Tweed" title="Berwick-upon-Tweed">Berwick-upon-Tweed</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stirling" title="Stirling">Stirling</a> and <a href="/wiki/Perth,_Scotland" title="Perth, Scotland">Perth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Justiciar" title="Justiciar">Justiciarship</a> too was created in David's reign. Although this institution had Anglo-Norman origins, in Scotland north of the Forth at least, it represented some form of continuity with an older office.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Economy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The revenue of his English earldom and the proceeds of the silver mines at <a href="/wiki/Alston,_Cumbria" title="Alston, Cumbria">Alston</a> allowed David to produce Scotland's first coinage. These altered the nature of trade and transformed his political image.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>David was a great town builder. As Prince of the Cumbrians, David founded the first two <a href="/wiki/Burghs" class="mw-redirect" title="Burghs">burghs</a> of "Scotland", at <a href="/wiki/Roxburgh" title="Roxburgh">Roxburgh</a> and Berwick.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Burghs were settlements with defined boundaries and guaranteed trading rights, locations where the king could collect and sell the products of his <i>cain</i> and <i>conveth</i> (a payment made <i>in lieu</i> of providing the king <a href="/wiki/Hospitality" title="Hospitality">hospitality</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> David founded around 15 burghs.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:MelroseAbbey01.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/MelroseAbbey01.jpg/220px-MelroseAbbey01.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/MelroseAbbey01.jpg/330px-MelroseAbbey01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/MelroseAbbey01.jpg/440px-MelroseAbbey01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="525" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:MelroseAbbey01.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The ruins of <a href="/wiki/Melrose_Abbey" title="Melrose Abbey">Melrose Abbey</a>. Founded in 1137, this Cistercian monastery became one of David's greatest legacies.</div></div></div>
<p>Perhaps nothing in David's reign compares in importance to burghs. While they could not, at first, have amounted to much more than the nucleus of an <a href="/wiki/Immigrant" class="mw-redirect" title="Immigrant">immigrant</a> <a href="/wiki/Merchant" title="Merchant">merchant</a> class, nothing would do more to reshape the long-term economic and ethnic shape of Scotland than the burgh. These planned towns were or dominated by <a href="/wiki/English_people" title="English people">English</a> in culture and language; <a href="/wiki/William_of_Newburgh" title="William of Newburgh">William of Newburgh</a> wrote in the reign of King <a href="/wiki/William_the_Lion" title="William the Lion">William the Lion</a>, that "the towns and burghs of the Scottish realm are known to be inhabited by English";<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> as well as transforming the economy, the dominance of an English influence would in the long term undermine the position of the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Irish_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Middle Irish language">Middle Irish language</a>, giving birth to the idea of the <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Lowlands" title="Scottish Lowlands">Scottish Lowlands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Monastic_patronage">Monastic patronage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Monastic patronage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>David was one of medieval Scotland's greatest monastic patrons. In 1113, in perhaps David's first act as Prince of the Cumbrians, he founded <a href="/wiki/Selkirk_Abbey" class="mw-redirect" title="Selkirk Abbey">Selkirk Abbey</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Tironensian" class="mw-redirect" title="Tironensian">Tironensians</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> David founded more than a dozen new monasteries in his reign, patronising various new monastic orders.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Not only were such monasteries an expression of David's undoubted piety, they also functioned to transform Scottish society. Monasteries became centres of foreign influence, and provided sources of <a href="/wiki/Literacy" title="Literacy">literate</a> men, able to serve the crown's growing administrative needs.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> These new monasteries, the <a href="/wiki/Cistercians" title="Cistercians">Cistercian</a> ones in particular, introduced new agricultural practices.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Cistercian labour, for instance, transformed southern Scotland into one of northern Europe's most important sources of sheep wool.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Genealogical_table">Genealogical table</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Genealogical table">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

</th></tr>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Fictional_portrayals">Fictional portrayals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Fictional portrayals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<ul><li><i>David the Prince</i> (1980) by <a href="/wiki/Nigel_Tranter" title="Nigel Tranter">Nigel Tranter</a>. The story of Queen Margaret's sons Alexander I and David I.</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=David_I_of_Scotland&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-columns references-column-width" style="column-width: 30em;">
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-1">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Modern <a href="/wiki/Scottish_Gaelic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Scottish Gaelic language">Scottish Gaelic</a> has effectively dropped the <i>Máel</i> in <i>Máel Coluim</i> (meaning "<a href="/wiki/Tonsure" title="Tonsure">tonsured</a> devotee of <a href="/wiki/Columba" title="Columba">Columba</a>"), so that the name is just <i>Colum</i> or <i>Calum</i> (meaning "Columba"); the name was borrowed into non Gaelic languages before this change occurred.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-2"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-2">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Thurston &amp; Attwater, <i>Butler’s Lives of the Saints</i>, pp. 383–4.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-3">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1133582631">.mw-parser-output cite.citation{font-style:inherit;word-wrap:break-word}.mw-parser-output .citation q{quotes:"\"""\"""'""'"}.mw-parser-output .citation:target{background-color:rgba(0,127,255,0.133)}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-free a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-free a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/65/Lock-green.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .id-lock-registration a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-limited a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-registration a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/d6/Lock-gray-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .id-lock-subscription a,.mw-parser-output .citation .cs1-lock-subscription a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/aa/Lock-red-alt-2.svg")right 0.1em center/9px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-ws-icon a{background:url("//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg")right 0.1em center/12px no-repeat}.mw-parser-output .cs1-code{color:inherit;background:inherit;border:none;padding:inherit}.mw-parser-output .cs1-hidden-error{display:none;color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-visible-error{color:#d33}.mw-parser-output .cs1-maint{display:none;color:#3a3;margin-left:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-format{font-size:95%}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-left{padding-left:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .cs1-kern-right{padding-right:0.2em}.mw-parser-output .citation .mw-selflink{font-weight:inherit}</style><cite class="citation web cs1 cs1-prop-foreign-lang-source"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://deon.pl/imiona-swietych/dawid,2108">"Dawid"</a>. <i>DEON.pl</i> (in Polish)<span class="reference-accessdate">. <li id="cite_note-5"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-5">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Malcolm seems to have had two sons before he married Margaret, presumably by <a href="/wiki/Ingibiorg_Finnsdottir" title="Ingibiorg Finnsdottir">Ingibiorg Finnsdottir</a>. <a href="/wiki/Duncan_II_of_Scotland" title="Duncan II of Scotland">Duncan II of Scotland</a> was one, and there was another called Domnall who died in 1085, see <i><a href="/wiki/Annals_of_Ulster" title="Annals of Ulster">Annals of Ulster</a></i>, s.a. 1085.2, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.ucc.ie/celt/published/G100001A/text656.html">here</a>; see also Oram, <i>David</i>, p. 23; and Duncan, <i>The Kingship of the Scots</i>, p. 55; the possibility that Máel Coluim had another son, also named Máel Coluim, is open, G. W. S. Barrow, "Malcolm III (d. 1093)".</span>
